### [1] [Learning Low-Order Approximations of the Quark Propagator in Lattice QCD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06612)
*Simon Pfahler,Maren Käfferlein,Daniel Knüttel,Christoph Lehner,Tilo Wettig*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: Introduces a gauge-equivariant neural network in the Clifford basis to discover convergent, interpretable quark propagator approximations—outperforming the divergent hopping expansion, especially for light quarks and finite-density regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The hopping expansion—a standard quark propagator approximation in lattice QCD—diverges for light quarks, hindering strong-coupling and finite-density simulations.

Method: An artificial scientific discovery framework using gauge-equivariant neural networks operating in the Clifford algebra basis to learn physically consistent, low-order approximations of the quark propagator.

Result: Discovered approximations are more accurate than the hopping expansion at equal computational cost; exhibit convergence where hopping fails; generalize across unseen gauge configurations and lattice volumes.

Conclusion: The framework yields plug-in-replaceable, interpretable approximations and suggests a novel convergent series expansion for the quark propagator, advancing both numerical simulation and theoretical understanding in lattice QCD.

Abstract: The hopping expansion is an important approximation of the quark propagator in lattice quantum chromodynamics, but it diverges for light quarks, which limits the scope of strong-coupling and finite-density simulations. We present an artificial scientific discovery framework using gauge-equivariant neural networks in the Clifford basis to extract interpretable approximations. Our approach yields improved low-order approximations that outperform the hopping expansion at identical computational cost. In the parameter regime where the hopping expansion fails, our approach identifies convergent behavior, thus suggesting a novel series expansion. The discovered approximations generalize to unseen gauge fields and volumes, enabling immediate implementation as plug-in replacements and a new theoretical understanding of quark-propagator expansions.

</details>

### [2] [Simultaneous measurement of the Higgs boson decay rates to WW and ZZ in fully hadronic final states at FCC-ee](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04397)
*Jan Eysermans,Mila Pandurovic,Christoph Paus,Michele Selvaggi,Aman Desai*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper studies Higgs boson decays to WW and ZZ in the Higgsstrahlung process at FCC-ee (240 GeV, 10.8 ab⁻¹), focusing on fully hadronic six-jet final states; it employs jet clustering and pairing to simultaneously extract σ(ZH)×BR with relative uncertainties of 1.48% (H→WW) and 8.20% (H→ZZ).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precise determination of Higgs couplings to W/Z bosons is essential for model-independent measurement of the Higgs total decay width, a central goal of the FCC-ee program.

Method: Analysis of Higgsstrahlung (e⁺e⁻ → ZH) events with fully hadronic decays: Z → qq̄ and H → WW/ZZ → 4q, yielding six-jet final states; simultaneous fit of WW and ZZ signals using optimized jet clustering and dedicated jet-pairing algorithms to resolve kinematic overlap.

Result: Expected relative uncertainties on σ(ZH) × BR are 1.48% for H → WW and 8.20% for H → ZZ, under FCC-ee baseline conditions (240 GeV, 10.8 ab⁻¹).

Conclusion: The fully hadronic six-jet channel provides competitive precision for H→WW and feasible sensitivity for H→ZZ at FCC-ee, enabling simultaneous, high-precision extraction of Higgs couplings to electroweak gauge bosons.

Abstract: The precise determination of the Higgs boson couplings to electroweak gauge bosons is a key objective of the FCC-ee physics program. In particular, measurements of the Higgs boson couplings to W and Z bosons play a central role in the model-independent determination of its total decay width. This paper presents a study of Higgs boson decays to H -> WW and H -> ZZ in the Higgsstrahlung production process. The analysis focuses on events in which the associated Z boson and the vector bosons from the Higgs decay all decay hadronically, resulting in six-jet final states. The large H -> WW branching fraction leads to high signal statistics in the fully hadronic channel, while for H -> ZZ the much smaller Higgs branching fraction is partly compensated by the large hadronic branching fractions of the Z bosons. Due to the substantial kinematic overlap between the WW and ZZ decay modes, their signal strengths are extracted simultaneously. Jet-clustering and dedicated jet-pairing techniques are employed to optimize the reconstruction of the hadronically decaying gauge bosons and enhance the separation of the two signal components. Assuming a center-of-mass energy of 240 GeV and an integrated luminosity of 10.8 ab^-1, expected relative uncertainties of 1.48% and 8.20% are obtained on the production cross section times branching fraction, sigma(ZH) x BR, for the H -> WW and H -> ZZ decay modes, respectively.

</details>


### [3] [Search for Higgs boson compositeness effects in high-mass dilepton final states in proton-proton collisions at $\sqrt{s}$ = 13 and 13.6 TeV](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04782)
*CMS Collaboration*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This CMS paper searches for Higgs boson compositeness via high-mass dielectron/dimuon spectra at 13/13.6 TeV, setting 95% CL limits on oblique parameters W (−2.1–1.6 × 10⁻⁴) and Y (−3.9–3.5 × 10⁻⁴); it also derives improved TeV-scale compositeness bounds assuming a physical Z′ resonance—including previously unexplored large-width scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To probe Higgs boson compositeness—a signature of physics beyond the Standard Model—by detecting distortions in dilepton mass spectra and new resonances (e.g., Z′), motivated by theoretical expectations that compositeness modifies electroweak precision observables via oblique parameters.

Method: Analysis of CMS data at √s = 13 and 13.6 TeV (207 fb⁻¹), using effective field theory to constrain oblique parameters W and Y via one-dimensional fits to dilepton invariant mass distributions; complementary search for Z′ resonances—including broad-width cases—within the observed mass spectrum.

Result: 95% CL limits: −2.1 × 10⁻⁴ < W < 1.6 × 10⁻⁴ and −3.9 × 10⁻⁴ < Y < 3.5 × 10⁻⁴; tighter compositeness constraints at the TeV scale under Z′ resonance hypotheses, including first exploration of large decay widths.

Conclusion: No significant deviation from SM predictions is observed; results place the most stringent direct CMS constraints to date on Higgs compositeness via oblique parameters and extend Z′-driven compositeness limits into previously untested broad-width regimes.

Abstract: A search for beyond the standard model effects in high-mass dielectron and dimuon final states with the CMS detector is performed, with a specific focus on Higgs boson compositeness. The search uses data collected at the LHC at 13 and 13.6 TeV center-of-mass energies, corresponding to a total integrated luminosity of 207 fb$^{-1}$. Compositeness would manifest as distortions in the observed dilepton mass spectrum and the presence of bound states, such as the Higgs boson and new massive vector boson resonances. Assuming the existence of new boson states with masses well above the TeV scale, an effective field theory approach provides stringent bounds on the oblique parameters $\mathcal{W}$ and $\mathcal{Y}$, which are zero in the standard model. One-dimensional fits, in which only one of the oblique parameters is allowed to deviate from zero, provide the following limits: $-$3.9 $\times$ 10$^{-4}$$\lt$ $\mathcal{Y}$ $\lt$ 3.5 $\times$ 10$^{-4}$ and $-$2.1 $\times$ 10$^{-4}$ $\lt$ $\mathcal{W}$ $\lt$ 1.6 $\times$ 10$^{-4}$ at 95% confidence level. More stringent compositeness limits at the TeV scale are set in several new physics scenarios under the assumption that a new Z' resonance is physically present within or slightly above the visible dilepton mass spectrum. In this context, cases with large resonance decay widths are explored for the first time.

</details>

K. Zhou,X. R. Zhou,X. Y. Zhou,Y. X. Zhou,Y. Z. Zhou,A. N. Zhu,J. Zhu,K. Zhu,K. J. Zhu,K. S. Zhu,L. X. Zhu,Lin Zhu,S. H. Zhu,T. J. Zhu,W. D. Zhu,W. J. Zhu,W. Z. Zhu,Y. C. Zhu,Z. A. Zhu,X. Y. Zhuang,M. Zhuge,J. H. Zou,J. Zu*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: First amplitude analysis of the doubly Cabibbo-suppressed decay D⁺ → K⁺π⁺π⁻π⁰ reveals first observations of D⁺ → K*⁰ρ⁺ and D⁺ → K*⁺ρ⁰, with precise branching fractions and polarization measurements; D⁺ → K⁺ω branching fraction is also updated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study doubly Cabibbo-suppressed decays (DCSDs) of charmed mesons, which are sensitive probes of strong dynamics, final-state interactions, and potential new physics; specifically, to resolve resonant substructures and polarization in the four-body decay D⁺ → K⁺π⁺π⁻π⁰.

Method: Amplitude analysis using a 20.3 fb⁻¹ e⁺e⁻ collision dataset at √s = 3.773 GeV collected by BESIII; full Dalitz plot and angular analyses to isolate intermediate vector-vector (VV) resonances (K*ρ, K*ω) and extract complex amplitudes, branching fractions, and polarization fractions.

Result: First observation of D⁺ → K*⁰ρ⁺ [ℬ = (5.67±0.41±0.17)×10⁻⁴] and D⁺ → K*⁺ρ⁰ [ℬ = (5.32±0.57±0.24)×10⁻⁴]; both exhibit large transverse polarization (longitudinal fractions f_L = 0.111±0.024±0.008 and 0.263±0.049±0.015); ℬ(D⁺ → K⁺ω) = (4.76±0.84±0.13)×10⁻⁵.

Conclusion: The results provide the first detailed dynamical picture of DCSD D⁺ → K⁺π⁺π⁻π⁰, confirm significant contributions from vector-vector final states with anomalous transverse dominance, and supply critical inputs for testing QCD-inspired models (e.g., factorization, pole dominance) and improving global fits of charm weak decays.

Abstract: By analyzing an $e^+e^-$ collision data sample with an integrated luminosity of 20.3 fb$^{-1}$ collected with the BESIII detector at the center-of-mass energy of 3.773 GeV, we perform the first amplitude analysis on the doubly Cabibbo-suppressed decay $D^+ \to K^+π^+π^-π^0$ and report the first observation of $D^+ \to K^{*0}ρ^+$ and $D^+\to K^{*+}ρ^0$. The corresponding branching fractions are $(5.67\pm0.41_{\rm stat}\pm0.17_{\rm syst})\times10^{-4}$ and $(5.32\pm0.57_{\rm stat}\pm0.24_{\rm syst})\times10^{-4}$, respectively. These two $D\to VV$ decay both have large transverse polarizations. The longitudinal polarization fractions are measured to be $0.111\pm0.024_{\rm stat}\pm0.008_{\rm syst}$ and $0.263\pm0.049_{\rm stat}\pm0.015_{\rm syst}$, respectively. The branching fraction of the decay $D^+\to K^+ω$ is measured to be $(4.76\pm0.84_{\rm stat}\pm0.13_{\rm syst})\times 10^{-5}$.

</details>


### [5] [Precision measurement of ocean tides using cosmic-ray muons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05294)
*Jiwon Seo,Jongseok Chung,Chang Hyon Ha,TongZhou Huang,Jinyoung Kim,Sooa Kim,Hani Kimku,Byoung-cheol Koh,Minsu Kwak,Sihyun Lee,Yujin Lee*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that cosmic-ray muons can be used to monitor ocean-tide-induced variations in seawater overburden within an underwater tunnel, achieving meter-scale resolution and quantitative agreement with tide-gauge data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a non-local, integrated measurement technique for time-dependent environmental mass variations—specifically, tidal changes in seawater overburden—complementing point-wise tide gauges.

Method: Muon flux measurements inside an underwater tunnel were performed continuously; temporal modulation of muon counts was correlated with independent tide-gauge records; attenuation response coefficient k was extracted via linear regression; a spatial muography scan was conducted to validate the overburden model including seawater contribution.

Result: Observed muon count modulation tracks tide-gauge data with attenuation coefficient k = 43.2 ± 1.0 counts/h/m; meter-scale overburden variations resolved; seawater contribution confirmed essential by muography-based overburden modeling.

Conclusion: Cosmic-ray muography enables quantitative, integrated monitoring of dynamic environmental mass distributions—here, tidal seawater—offering a novel geophysical sensing modality beyond conventional local instrumentation.

Abstract: Cosmic-ray muons provide a natural probe of the integrated mass along their trajectories. Here we show that cosmic-ray muons can monitor ocean-tide-induced variations in seawater overburden within an underwater tunnel. The observed modulation closely tracks independent tide-gauge measurements, yielding an attenuation response coefficient of $k=43.2\pm1.0$~counts$\rm /h/m$ and resolving meter-scale variations in seawater overburden. A spatial muography scan validates the overburden model, confirming that the seawater contribution is essential to reproduce the observed attenuation. Unlike conventional tide gauges that measure sea level locally, muography measures the integrated overburden above the detector. These results demonstrate that muography can quantitatively resolve small, time-dependent variations in environmental overburden, providing a new approach to monitoring dynamic natural systems.

</details>


### [6] [Search for Diffuse Supernova Neutrino Background of All Flavors with PandaX-4T](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05980)
*Yingxin Zhang,PandaX Collaboration*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper reports the first search for the diffuse supernova neutrino background (DSNB) using coherent elastic neutrino-nucleus scattering (CEνNS) in the PandaX-4T liquid xenon detector, yielding a competitive 90% CL upper limit on the DSNB ν_x flux above 19.3 MeV.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To probe the diffuse supernova neutrino background—a cosmological flux of neutrinos from all past core-collapse supernovae—using CEνNS, a low-threshold interaction channel offering enhanced sensitivity to MeV-scale neutrinos in heavy nuclei.

Method: Analysis of 1.54 tonne·year exposure from PandaX-4T's commissioning and first science runs; background modeling and statistical inference to set an upper limit on the DSNB ν_x flux via CEνNS signal expectation above 19.3 MeV.

Result: No significant excess observed; 90% CL upper limit of (2.27–2.43) × 10³ cm⁻² s⁻¹ per flavor (ν_μ, ν̄_μ, ν_τ, ν̄_τ) for E_ν > 19.3 MeV.

Conclusion: The PandaX-4T experiment establishes the first CEνNS-based constraint on the DSNB, achieving sensitivity comparable to the world’s best limits, demonstrating the viability of large-scale liquid xenon detectors for MeV neutrino astrophysics.

Abstract: We present the first search for the diffuse supernova neutrino background utilizing coherent elastic neutrino-nucleus scattering with the PandaX-4T liquid xenon detector at the China Jinping Underground Laboratory. Based on a total exposure of $1.54~\mathrm{tonne \cdot year}$ from the commissioning and first science runs, we observe no significant event excess over the expected background. We therefore set an upper limit of $(2.27 - 2.43) \times 10^3~\mathrm{cm^{-2}s^{-1}}$ per flavor at 90\% confidence level on the $ν_x$ flux (each of $ν_μ, \barν_μ, ν_τ, \barν_τ$) of the diffuse supernova neutrino background for incident neutrino energies above 19.3~MeV. The result is comparable to the current world best limit.

</details>


### [7] [A graph neural network for deep learning-based full event interpretation at FCC-ee](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06054)
*Elena Graverini,Samuel Morand,Rafael Silva Coutinho,Felipe Luan Souza de Almeida*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper introduces a graph neural network (GNN) framework for full event interpretation in FCC-ee $e^+e^- \rightarrow Z \rightarrow b\bar{b}$ events, achieving >90% accuracy in reconstructing correct $b$-hadron decay chains and >93% clustering efficiency for multi-generation decays using IDEA detector simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable precise flavor physics and Higgs coupling measurements at FCC-ee, robust reconstruction of complex $b$-hadron decay topologies is required; traditional reconstruction algorithms struggle with combinatorial ambiguity and hierarchical decay structures.

Method: A graph neural network operating on particle-level graphs: charged particle tracks serve as nodes; pairwise kinematic and geometric observables (e.g., angular separation, invariant mass proxies) are edge features; message-passing layers learn hierarchical clustering to infer decay trees without explicit intermediate resonance reconstruction.

Result: On simulated $Z \rightarrow b\bar{b}$ events, 90% of reconstructed decay chains contain exactly the correct particle set (regardless of hierarchy); clustering efficiency exceeds 93% across benchmark $b$-hadron decays with up to three decay generations.

Conclusion: The GNN-based full event interpretation framework demonstrates high fidelity in reconstructing $b$-hadron decay trees directly from charged-particle information, validating its suitability for precision flavor physics at future $e^+e^-$ colliders like FCC-ee with the IDEA detector concept.

Abstract: This paper presents a deep learning-based full event interpretation prototype developed for the electron-positron Future Circular Collider (FCC-$ee$) using simulated $e^{+}e^{-}\rightarrow Z\rightarrow b\bar{b}$ events at $\sqrt{s} = 91 \ \mathrm{GeV}$. The study was carried out using the Innovative Detector for Electron positron Accelerators (IDEA) detector concept as case study for future application. The proposed graph neural network framework aims to simultaneously identify and reconstruct the hierarchical decay trees of $b$-hadrons in each event using information from reconstructed charged particles. Events are represented as graphs, where reconstructed charged particles form the nodes and pairwise observables are encoded as edge features describing the relations between particles. On a test sample, 90% of reconstructed decay chains contain exactly the correct set of particles, irrespective of their inferred decay hierarchy. Across a range of benchmark $b$-hadron decays, this clustering efficiency exceeds 93% for decay chains spanning up to three generations of decays.

</details>


### [8] [SysVar: Consistent and scalable treatment of systematic uncertainties in template-based analyses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06117)
*Ilias Tsaklidis,Agrim Aggarwal,Georgios Alexandris,Tristan Fillinger,Giacomo De Pietro,Daniel Ivanov,Melisa-Melek Akdag,Markus Prim,Florian Bernlochner*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: SysVar is a lightweight, open-source Python package that automates propagation of correlated systematic uncertainties from correction weights into histogram templates, preserving full covariance via orthogonal eigenvariations for robust high-precision signal extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling analyses face growing challenges in correctly propagating systematic uncertainties from correction weights into simulated templates while preserving correlations among signal extraction variables—errors in manual bookkeeping compromise precision and reliability.

Method: SysVar introduces a streamlined API that automates weight application, systematic variation construction, and histogramming; it preserves correlations by propagating the full covariance matrix across the analysis and compressing it into a minimal set of orthogonal eigenvariations, each mapped to a standard shape nuisance parameter.

Result: SysVar enables consistent, correlated systematic treatment across independent pseudo-measurements, demonstrated through their combination; it reduces error-prone manual workflows and supports experiment-agnostic, pip-installable deployment.

Conclusion: SysVar significantly improves scalability, reproducibility, and robustness of template-based statistical analyses in HEP by unifying systematic uncertainty propagation with correlation preservation in a lightweight, open framework.

Abstract: Propagating systematic uncertainties from correction weights into templates generated from simulated data while preserving correlations in the signal extraction variables becomes increasingly challenging as analyses scale in size. SysVar's lightweight API hides most of the bookkeeping complexity of applying correction weights, building systematic variations, and histogramming nominal and varied templates. This streamlines workflows that are otherwise error-prone but essential for high-precision measurements. Correlations are preserved by propagating the full covariance across the analysis and compressing it into a small set of orthogonal eigenvariations, each defining an ordinary shape nuisance parameter. In this work, we demonstrate SysVar's workflow and impact by combining two independent pseudo-measurements that benefit from consistently correlated systematic uncertainties encoded in the template shapes. SysVar is open-source, pip-installable, and experiment-agnostic.

</details>


### [9] [FPGA-based muon shower identification and graph neural network tracking algorithms for HL-LHC triggers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06379)
*Daniel Estrada,Elena Aller,Andrea Cardini,Santiago Folgueras,Pelayo Leguina,Javier Prado*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper introduces two FPGA-based hardware-accelerated strategies for upgrading the CMS Level-1 Trigger: (1) a low-latency muon shower identification algorithm using Drift Tube hit multiplicities in the barrel, and (2) a quantized GraphSAGE-based GNN for displaced muon tracking in the barrel-endcap overlap region, achieving 20% DSP usage and 19-cycle deterministic latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To recover L1T efficiency losses caused by highly energetic radiating muons and unconventional signatures—especially Long-Lived Particles (LLPs)—which conventional track finders miss due to non-standard trajectories and delayed energy deposition.

Method: First strategy: A custom, ultra-low-resource FPGA algorithm monitoring hit multiplicities in CMS Drift Tube chambers to generate Shower Primitives with sub-bunch-crossing latency. Second strategy: A hardware-software co-designed Graph Neural Network (GraphSAGE proxy) deployed on FPGAs using INT8-PO2 quantization, data-driven bit-width optimization, and replacement of DSP-intensive multiplications with bit-shifts.

Result: Shower Primitive generation achieves sub-bunch-crossing latency and enables tagging of showered muons; GNN implementation reduces DSP utilization to 20% and delivers deterministic 19-clock-cycle inference latency while maintaining physics viability in the overlap region.

Conclusion: Both strategies are feasible, resource-efficient, and latency-compliant FPGA accelerators that extend CMS L1T capabilities to non-standard muon signatures—particularly those from LLP decays—without compromising real-time trigger constraints.

Abstract: This work presents two hardware-accelerated strategies for the upgrade of the CMS Level-1 Trigger (L1T) to target efficiency losses due to highly energetic radiating muons and unconventional physics signatures, such as Long-Lived Particles (LLPs). Operating directly on low-level detector hits, the first strategy implements a dedicated muon shower identification algorithm in the CMS barrel region that monitors hit multiplicities in the Drift Tube chambers to generate Shower Primitives. Executing with sub-bunch-crossing latency and ultra-low resource consumption, this algorithm successfully tags showered muons and provides essential context for downstream track finders. The second approach targets the barrel-endcap transition (overlap) region by evaluating Graph Neural Networks to reconstruct displaced muon tracks. Following a divided methodology that first isolates hardware feasibility from evolving physics refinements, an initial proxy model based on a GraphSAGE architecture is evaluated to validate its viability on FPGAs within strict latency limits. This implementation utilizes an INT8-PO2 quantization technique coupled with a data-driven bit-width optimization. By replacing DSP-heavy fixed-point multiplications with fast compile-time arithmetic bit-shifts and allocating the minimum required bits per signal, this hardware-software co-design reduces DSP utilization to $20\%$ and achieves a deterministic inference latency of just $19$ clock cycles. Together, these developments pave the way for triggering on non-standard muon signatures, such as those from LLP decays, at L1T.

</details>


### [10] [Design optimization of the inter-electrode structures in DC-RSD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06676)
*N. Cartiglia,R. Arcidiacono,G. Bardelli,M. Boscardin,A. Cassese,M. Centis Vignali,T. Croci,M. Durando,M. Ferrero,A. Fondacci,S. Gaiero,O. Hammad Ali,L. Lanteri,A. Losana,L. Menzio,A. Morozzi,F. Moscatelli,D. Passeri,G. Paternoster,G. Sguazzoni,F. Siviero,V. Sola,L. Viliani*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper compares inter-electrode structures (trenches, resistive strips, no structure) in C-coupled Resistive Silicon Detectors (RSDs) using a custom 2D Kirchhoff-network simulator (DCRSD_SurfaceSimulator), validated on test-beam data; it finds resistive strips yield ~25% better spatial resolution and superior gradient uniformity in charge sharing than trenches, due to stronger and more uniform spatial gradients in the charge-sharing fraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine which inter-electrode structure — trench, resistive strip, or none — optimally improves spatial resolution and its uniformity in C-coupled Resistive Silicon Detectors (RSDs).

Method: Developed DCRSD_SurfaceSimulator, a ROOT-based 2D Kirchhoff resistive network model simulating charge sharing as a function of hit position, surface resistivity, and inter-electrode geometry; validated against test-beam data from FBK DC-RSD sensors (300–1000 μm pitch); performed comparative analysis of trenches, resistive strips, and no containment across resolution, uniformity, inter-electrode resistance, and signal leakage.

Result: Resistive strips outperform trenches by ~25% in spatial resolution across tested strip resistances (500–2000 Ω) and input impedances (0–100 Ω) at 1000 Ω/□ surface resistivity; this gain stems from a stronger and more uniform spatial gradient in the charge-sharing fraction, especially away from pixel centers.

Conclusion: Resistive strips are the optimal inter-electrode structure for maximizing spatial resolution and uniformity in C-coupled RSDs under the studied conditions, with performance advantage rooted in superior charge-sharing gradient characteristics compared to trenches or unstructured surfaces.

Abstract: C-coupled Resistive Silicon Detectors (RSD) share charge among electrodes through a resistive n$^+$ surface layer, and the choice of inter-electrode structure --- a trench, a resistive strip, or no structure at all --- directly determines the achievable position resolution and its uniformity. This paper addresses the question of which structure best optimises the spatial resolution. To answer it quantitatively, DCRSD_SurfaceSimulator was developed, a ROOT-based tool that models the resistive surface as a two-dimensional Kirchhoff resistive network and solves for charge sharing as a function of hit position, surface resistivity, and inter-electrode structure. The simulator is validated against test-beam data from FBK DC-RSD sensors with 300, 500, and 1000~$μ$m pixel pitch, reproducing the position resolution, its spatial uniformity, and the signal-sharing templates correctly. Using the validated simulator, trenches, resistive strips, and no containment are then compared on position resolution and its spatial uniformity, inter-electrode resistance, and signal leakage to outer pixels. Resistive strips are found to outperform trenches in resolution by approximately 25\% for all tested strip resistances (500--2000~$Ω$) and input impedances (0--100~$Ω$), at a surface resistivity of 1000~$Ω/\square$, and trace the origin of this difference to the spatial gradient of the charge-sharing fraction: trenches produce a markedly weaker and less uniform gradient, particularly away from the pixel centre, than either a resistive strip or a bare resistive surface.

</details>


### [11] [How to scale your HEP ML models: A recipe for robust architecture comparisons at scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06784)
*Matthias Vigl,Nikita Pond,Jackson Barr,Alexander Froch,Dan Guest,Nicole Hartman,Michael Kagan,Lukas Heinrich*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper establishes robust scaling laws for high-energy physics (HEP) machine learning tasks—particularly jet classification—by systematically analyzing performance vs. compute and data budgets, validating on toy problems and the 11B-jet ATLAS JetSet2 dataset; it identifies optimal hyperparameters under early stopping, confirms √C scaling in compute-optimal regime, shows benefit of auxiliary objectives and low-level inputs, and emphasizes necessity of large full-simulation datasets for reliable scaling behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable systematic, reproducible scaling studies in high-energy physics—where recent empirical scaling behavior has emerged but lacks standardized methodology—bridging the gap with established practices in language modeling.

Method: A systematic procedure for deriving robust scaling laws: (1) validation on controlled toy problems; (2) application to multi-task transformers on ATLAS JetSet2 (~11B jets); (3) separate analysis in compute-constrained and data-constrained regimes; (4) joint hyperparameter optimization (model size, training horizon, learning rate, batch size) under early stopping; (5) ablation of input granularity and auxiliary objectives.

Result: In data-constrained regime: first prediction of jointly optimal hyperparameters under early stopping. In compute-optimal regime: near-equal √C scaling of model and dataset size; auxiliary objectives reduce primary loss at fixed compute; expanding to lower-level inputs reduces loss without altering scaling exponent; power-law scaling emerges only above a critical dataset size threshold.

Conclusion: Robust scaling laws in HEP require large, high-quality full-simulation datasets; the proposed methodology enables principled foundation model development, hyperparameter co-design, and transferable insights across physics-informed ML tasks.

Abstract: Much of the recent progress in machine learning domains such as language models has come from scaling laws that predict performance as a function of training effort. In high-energy physics (HEP) similar behavior has now been observed. To aid further study, we present a systematic procedure to derive robust scaling laws and compare design choices on the relevant budget axes for HEP tasks. We first validate the full scaling trajectory on toy problems and then apply the procedure to multi-task transformers on the ~11 billion-jet ATLAS JetSet2 dataset, in both the compute- and data-constrained regimes. For the latter, we predict, to the best of our knowledge for the first time, the jointly optimal model size, training horizon, learning rate and batch size under early stopping. At compute-optimal scaling, we recover a near-equal $\sqrt{C}$ dependence of model and dataset size, and find that auxiliary objectives lower the primary jet-classification loss at equal compute budget. Expanding the inputs toward lower-level data systematically lowers the loss while leaving the scaling exponent nearly unchanged. The onset of the power-law regime is itself set by scale: below a threshold in dataset size the loss carries little information about high-compute scaling, underscoring the value of large, high-quality full-simulation datasets as a foundation for scaling studies and the development of foundation models in HEP.

</details>


### [12] [Reconfigurable On-Chip AI for Particle Physics Detectors with Logic Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06816)
*Mila Bileska,Lino Gerlach,Jennet Dickinson,Lindsey Gray,Giuseppe Di Guglielmo,Peter Elmer,Isobel Ojalvo*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper evaluates Logic Neural Networks (LNNs) for on-chip inference in next-generation particle physics pixel detectors, demonstrating their superior hardware efficiency, radiation robustness, and—critically—their reconfigurability to adapt post-deployment to radiation-induced detector degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Next-generation pixel detectors in particle physics face extreme data rates, strict power/space constraints, and radiation-induced parameter drift, necessitating highly efficient, robust, and *reconfigurable* on-device ML models—beyond static hard-wired solutions.

Method: The authors evaluate LNNs for on-chip inference in pixel detectors, analyze their resource efficiency and radiation tolerance (specifically to random bit flips), and introduce novel architectural and training techniques enabling post-deployment reconfiguration of LNNs.

Result: LNNs enable more complex models within tight hardware budgets; exhibit increased robustness against radiation-induced bit flips; and—via novel reconfigurable designs—support model adaptation to changing detector conditions after deployment.

Conclusion: LNNs are a promising foundation for adaptive, radiation-hardened, on-chip ML in high-energy physics detectors, bridging the gap between static hardware efficiency and dynamic model evolution.

Abstract: On-device machine learning is increasingly important in applications where extreme data rates or resource constraints make centralized processing infeasible. Logic neural networks (LNNs), which directly learn Boolean logic instead of conventional arithmetic operations, have recently emerged as a state-of-the-art approach for efficient inference and are particularly well suited for deployment in custom silicon. However, hard-wired solutions are typically optimized for narrowly defined tasks and lack the ability to adapt to changing data distributions. Next-generation pixel detectors in particle physics present a particularly challenging use case: their high granularity and readout frequency generate data rates that cannot be centrally processed, while stringent space and power constraints necessitate highly efficient on-chip inference. Moreover, radiation damage progressively alters detector response, requiring models that can be re-trained and reconfigured after deployment. In this work, we evaluate LNNs for on-chip inference in pixel detectors. We show that their superior resource efficiency may enable a step change in model capability, making more complex architectures feasible within the available hardware budget. LNNs also provide increased robustness against radiation-induced random bit flips. Finally, we present novel work on reconfigurable LNNs, enabling models to adapt to changing operating conditions after deployment.

</details>

### [13] [Status and Progress of Hamiltonian Effective Field Theory in Light Hadron Spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06075)
*Qi-Liang Xie,Qiu-Rui Wu,Zi-Jun Li,Fang-Chao Han,Yu Zhuge,Zhan-Wei Liu*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: This review introduces Hamiltonian effective field theory (HEFT) as a unified framework to describe hadron resonances by bridging quark-model states, hadronic channels, experimental scattering data, and lattice QCD results—enabling discrimination between competing dynamical interpretations of light-hadron spectra.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve ambiguities in interpreting hadron resonance structure—specifically, disentangling contributions from bare quark-model states versus dynamical multihadron configurations—using a consistent Hamiltonian-based formalism that integrates both experimental and first-principles lattice QCD inputs.

Method: Hamiltonian effective field theory (HEFT), formulated in both infinite- and finite-volume settings; combines model-space Hamiltonians with continuum couplings to hadronic channels; constrained simultaneously by experimental scattering observables and lattice QCD energy spectra.

Result: HEFT enables quantitative discrimination among phenomenologically equivalent scattering descriptions by revealing distinct predictions for bare-state content, resonance pole positions, and finite-volume spectrum shifts; recent extensions accommodate coupled-channel systems, inelastic thresholds, and multi-hadron decay dynamics.

Conclusion: HEFT provides a robust, predictive, and systematically improvable framework for hadron spectroscopy, uniquely positioned to unify lattice QCD, experiment, and theoretical models in elucidating the dynamical origin of resonances.

Abstract: Hadron resonances are intrinsically dynamical states whose observable properties are governed by the interplay between quark-model configurations and hadronic channels. Hamiltonian effective field theory provides a practical framework for connecting hadron dynamics with experimental observables and lattice QCD results. In this review, we outline the infinite- and finite-volume formulations of Hamiltonian effective field theory and survey its applications to light-hadron spectroscopy and resonance structure. By constraining the same Hamiltonian framework with experimental scattering data and lattice QCD spectra, Hamiltonian effective field theory can help distinguish among scenarios that describe the available scattering data comparably well but imply different roles for bare-state and multihadron configurations. We also review recent extensions of the Hamiltonian framework developed to address more complicated dynamical settings.

</details>


### [14] [Phenomenological Estimate for the Longitudinal Unpolarized Structure Function in Semi-inclusive Deep Inelastic Scattering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06628)
*Alessandro Bacchetta,Matteo Cerutti,Leonard Gamberg,Richard Whitehill*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a phenomenological study of the longitudinal unpolarized structure function $F_{UU,L}$ and its ratio $R_{\, \rm SIDIS}=F_{UU,L}/F_{UU,T}$ in semi-inclusive deep inelastic scattering (SIDIS), emphasizing transverse-momentum dependence and next-to-next-to-leading-power contributions at low $q_T$, finding $R_{\, \rm SIDIS} \simeq 0.3$ with ~10% impact on integrated observables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the phenomenological relevance of the longitudinal unpolarized structure function $F_{UU,L}$ in SIDIS—particularly its often-neglected low-transverse-momentum contribution—and to quantify its impact on transverse-momentum-integrated observables and multiplicity interpretations.

Method: Phenomenological analysis combining low-$q_T$ (TMD factorization with next-to-next-to-leading-power corrections) and high-$q_T$ (collinear factorization) regimes across the full SIDIS kinematic range; numerical estimation of $R_{\, \rm SIDIS}$ and its effect on integrated cross sections.

Result: $F_{UU,L}$ is power-suppressed at low $q_T$, yet yields a non-negligible $R_{\, \rm SIDIS} \simeq 0.3$ at low $q_T$ and hard scale, inducing ~10% corrections to transverse-momentum-integrated observables; theoretical uncertainties remain, but the effect motivates dedicated experimental measurements of $R_{\, \rm SIDIS}$.

Conclusion: The longitudinal structure function $F_{UU,L}$—though power-suppressed—has a measurable impact on SIDIS observables; its inclusion is likely essential for precision extractions of fragmentation functions and TMDs, and $R_{\, \rm SIDIS}$ warrants dedicated experimental study.

Abstract: We present a phenomenological study of the longitudinal unpolarized structure function $F_{UU,L}$ in semi-inclusive deep inelastic scattering (SIDIS) and its ratio with the transverse structure function, $R_{\, \rm SIDIS}=F_{UU,L}/F_{UU,T}$. We study their transverse-momentum dependence, combining for the first time low- and high-transverse-momentum contributions across the kinematic range relevant to SIDIS measurements. At low transverse momentum, $F_{UU,L}$ is power suppressed, making it sensitive to next-to-next-to-leading-power dynamics in the unpolarized sector. We find that this contribution can be sizable, with $R_{\, \rm SIDIS}\simeq 0.3$ at low transverse momentum and hard scale, producing a correction of the order of $10\%$ to transverse-momentum-integrated observables. Although these estimates remain subject to theoretical uncertainties, our results indicate that accounting for $F_{UU,L}$ is likely to be important for the interpretation of SIDIS multiplicities and motivate dedicated measurements of $R_{\, \rm SIDIS}$ to better constrain the longitudinal contribution.

</details>


### [15] [Two-loop RI/SMOM-$\overline{\text{MS}}$ matching factors for the renormalization of bi-local operators contributing to $ε_K$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06734)
*Erik Lundstrum*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: This paper computes two-loop QCD matching factors needed to connect lattice-regularized RI/SMOM renormalized operators to the continuum MS-bar scheme, essential for a first-principles lattice QCD calculation of the long-distance contribution to ε_K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate Standard Model prediction of ε_K requires non-perturbative evaluation of its long-distance ΔS = 2 contribution via two ΔS = 1 operators; this necessitates precise renormalization matching between lattice (RI/SMOM) and continuum (MS-bar) schemes.

Method: Two-loop perturbative QCD calculation of RI/SMOM to MS-bar matching factors for the relevant four-quark operators, performed in dimensional regularization with MS-bar subtraction.

Result: Complete set of O(α_s) matching coefficients for the four-quark operators mediating the long-distance ε_K contribution, enabling consistent conversion from RI/SMOM-renormalized lattice matrix elements to the MS-bar scheme.

Conclusion: The computed matching factors are a necessary ingredient for reducing systematic uncertainty in lattice QCD determinations of ε_K’s long-distance part, thereby improving the precision test of CKM unitarity and probing for new physics.

Abstract: The measure of indirect CP violation in the neutral kaon system, $ε_K$, is an important precision test of the Standard Model. A first-principles Standard Model prediction of $ε_K$ requires evaluating the long-distance contribution in which the $ΔS = 2$ process proceeds via two $ΔS = 1$ operators. The non-perturbative nature of QCD in the relevant energy regimes requires a lattice QCD simulation. In this paper we present the two-loop, $O(α_s)$ RI/SMOM - $\overline{\text{MS}}$ matching factors required to renormalize a lattice QCD calculation of the long-distance contribution to $ε_K$.

</details>


### [16] [An optimal observable for polarization measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03855)
*Erik Bachmann,Mareen Hoppe*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces parton-level differential polarization fractions as optimal observables for measuring vector-boson polarization, enabling maximal sensitivity to new physics in the electroweak sector; it shows these observables retain all experimentally accessible polarization information, can be learned via neural networks, and combine polarization sensitivity with model independence—demonstrated in inclusive ZZ production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vector-boson polarization is a sensitive probe of new physics in the electroweak sector, but rare processes and suppressed longitudinal contributions necessitate optimal polarization separation to fully exploit limited data.

Method: The authors define parton-level differential polarization fractions as optimal observables and fundamental sensitivity limits for polarized cross-section measurements; they prove that conditional expectations of these fractions retain all experimentally accessible polarization information and propose neural-network-based learning for unbinned or binned inference.

Result: The proposed observables enable measurement of polarization-sensitive distributions while preserving the model independence of fiducial cross sections, allowing detection of deviations beyond simple rescalings of polarized components; validated in inclusive ZZ production.

Conclusion: Parton-level differential polarization fractions constitute theoretically optimal, experimentally realizable observables for vector-boson polarization studies, unifying statistical optimality, model independence, and practical feasibility via ML-enhanced inference.

Abstract: Vector-boson polarization provides a sensitive probe of new physics in the electroweak sector. Rare processes and suppressed longitudinal contributions make optimal polarization separation essential to exploit the available data. We show that parton-level differential polarization fractions define optimal observables and a fundamental sensitivity limit for polarized cross-section measurements. Their conditional expectations retain all experimentally accessible polarization information, yielding the best achievable sensitivity within the assumed model, and can be learned by neural networks for unbinned or binned inference. Measuring their distributions combines polarization sensitivity with the model independence of fiducial cross sections, probing deviations beyond constant rescalings of polarized contributions. We demonstrate the approach in inclusive ZZ production.

</details>


### [17] [No Einstein Relation, No Problem: How Relativistic Weakly Coupled Heavy Quarks Equilibrate Beyond Leading Logarithm Anyway](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03863)
*Jean F. Du Plessis,Bruno Scheihing-Hitschfeld*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies how relativistic heavy quarks equilibrate in both weakly and strongly coupled plasmas, revealing that non-Gaussian momentum-transfer kernels—characterized by exponential tails and bounded analyticity domains—play a decisive role; despite quantitative differences in singularity structure and velocity dependence between weak-coupling QCD-like and strong-coupling N=4 SYM regimes, both share qualitative features enabling a unified phenomenological framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how relativistic heavy quarks equilibrate in quark-gluon plasmas despite violations of the Einstein relation, and to bridge microscopic transport (drag/diffusion) with macroscopic spectral evolution across coupling regimes.

Method: Computation of the momentum-transfer kernel at strict O(g⁴) in weakly coupled non-Abelian plasma; analysis of its analytic structure, large-order cumulant asymptotics, and tail behavior; comparison with strongly coupled N=4 SYM using known holographic results; leading-order-in-inverse-mass kinetic evolution analysis.

Result: The momentum-transfer kernel is intrinsically non-Gaussian with asymmetric exponential tails fixed by its bounded domain of analyticity; weak coupling exhibits drag-dominated survivor bias suppression, while strong coupling shows fluctuation-induced enhancement of relativistic population; both regimes share Gaussian core + exponential tails structure but differ in singularity type and velocity scaling of transport coefficients.

Conclusion: Non-Gaussian kernel structure governs relativistic heavy-quark equilibration dynamics across couplings; shared qualitative features—despite quantitative differences—support developing a first-principles–anchored phenomenological model for heavy-quark energy loss in heavy-ion collisions.

Abstract: Relativistic heavy quarks equilibrate even when their microscopic drag and momentum diffusion violate the Einstein relation. We investigate how equilibration proceeds as a function of the coupling and compared to simplified Fokker-Planck models. To do this, we present a detailed calculation of the momentum-transfer kernel in weakly coupled non-Abelian plasmas through strict $\mathcal{O}(g^4)$, and study the resulting dynamics at leading order in the inverse heavy-quark mass. Beyond leading logarithm, its non-Gaussian structure plays a crucial role in equilibration. We examine closely the analytic structure of the evolution kernel, determine the large-order asymptotics of the momentum-transfer cumulants, and show how the kernel's bounded analytic domain fixes asymmetric exponential tails at the level of the momentum transfer probability. Strongly coupled $\mathcal{N}=4$ SYM also features an approximately Gaussian core and exponential tails, but differs in the nature of the kernel's singularities and the velocity dependence of the transport coefficients and tail exponents. These differences have distinct dynamical consequences: for the parameters studied, the persistence of the relativistic population in steeply falling spectra is largely explained by drag alone at very weak coupling, whereas fluctuation-induced survivor bias produces a large relative enhancement at strong coupling. Nevertheless, the shared qualitative structure between weak and strong coupling points the way towards a practical phenomenological description of heavy quarks anchored in first-principles field theory.

</details>


### [18] [A Powheg generator for $t$- and $s$-channel single-top production and decay including non-resonant effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03878)
*Melissa van Beekveld,Robin van Bijleveld,Silvia Ferrario Ravasio,Tomas Jezo,Tommaso Saracco*

Main category: hep-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a NLO+PS generator for single-top production (t- and s-channel) in hadron colliders, implemented in POWHEG BOX RES, modeling $pp \to \ell^\pm \nu j_b j$ with full off-shell and non-resonant effects, interfaced to Pythia for resonance-aware showering, and combining 5-flavour scheme accuracy with bottom-mass effects in jets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a precise, publicly available Monte Carlo generator for single-top production that consistently includes off-shell and non-resonant contributions, handles top quark decays correctly in parton shower matching, and reconciles massless 5-flavour calculations with physical b-jet mass effects.

Method: Implemented a NLO QCD calculation matched to parton shower via the POWHEG BOX RES framework; included full spin correlations, off-shell W/top propagators, non-resonant diagrams, and dedicated interface to Pythia accounting for decaying resonances; employed fiducial phase-space cuts for phenomenological validation.

Result: A fully functional, public POWHEG BOX RES generator for t- and s-channel single-top with leptonic top decay; validated shower effects and fiducial cross sections; demonstrated consistent treatment of b-jet mass effects within a 5-flavour scheme.

Conclusion: The generator achieves a robust, high-precision description of single-top production with leptonic decays, enabling accurate LHC measurements and BSM searches by unifying NLO accuracy, resonance-aware showering, and realistic b-quark mass effects in final-state jets.

Abstract: We present a next-to-leading order + parton shower generator for $t$- and $s$-channel single-top production in hadron colliders, for a leptonically-decaying top, including off-shell and non-resonant effects. The generator, publicly available in the POWHEG BOX RES framework, describes the hadro-production of a $b$-jet and a leptonically decaying $W$-boson, accompanied by an additional jet, i.e. $pp\to \ell^{+} ν_{\ell}\, j_b\, j $ and $pp\to \ell^{-} \barν_{\ell}\, j_{\bar{b}}\, j $, where $j_b$ ($j_{\bar{b}}$) is a jet containing a bottom (anti)quark, and $j$ is a generic jet. We also provide an interface to the Pythia parton shower, suited to matching in the presence of decaying resonances such as the top quark. Our matching is designed to combine the advantages of a five-flavour calculation with the description of bottom quark mass effects in final-state jets. We perform a phenomenological study using fiducial cuts and discuss shower effects.

</details>


### [19] [Reinterpreting ATLAS Dijet and Supersymmetry Searches in A Baryon-Number-Violating Heavy Scalar Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03954)
